Weekly self-reports from P1 and researcher-led interviews with P2 provide descriptive observations of early use and perceived naturalness. The pilot does not support claims about clinical efficacy, diagnosis, therapeutic outcomes, general usability, or deployment safety. Relic provides research infrastructure intended to make relational memory, safety constraints, and agent behavior inspectable and testable.

cross MeshRT: Compile-Time Governed Wafer-Scale Runtime for Low-Latency High-Throughput Inference

Authors: Congjie He, Le Xu, Zhan Lu, Yeqi Huang, Haocheng Xiao, Cheng Deng, Lingxiao Ma, Ziming Miao, Fan Yang, Luo Mai

Abstract: Wafer-scale accelerators promise ultra-low-latency AI inference, but current system stacks still carry an unsustainably high cost premium. The reason is that many current and emerging inference techniques, such as batching and MoE, introduce runtime dynamism that existing wafer-scale systems cannot support efficiently. As a result, they must choose between two unsatisfactory options: accept suboptimal latency, or preserve low latency by restricting dynamic techniques, thereby limiting the models they can support and reducing aggregate throughput. This tradeoff significantly increases the cost of low-latency serving. We present MeshRT, the first wafer-scale system to achieve both ultra-low latency and high throughput for LLM inference. MeshRT achieves this via a novel system architecture, which we call compile-time governance of runtime dynamism. This architecture features new system abstractions and mechanisms that enable MeshRT to compile dynamic events arising from LLM inference directly into per-core schedules, enabling asynchronous and decentralized execution with low runtime overhead. Implemented on a commodity wafer-scale accelerator, MeshRT preserves ultra-low latency while improving throughput by up to 56$\times$ over the state-of-the-art system for wafer-scale accelerators. Against measured single-instance SGLang results on H200, MeshRT achieves 4.5-7.0$\times$ lower latency. Under ideal linear scaling of those GPU measurements to an approximately power-matched H200 cluster, MeshRT achieves 1.7-13.0$\times$ higher decode system throughput in the low-latency regime.

cross MorphSched: Workload-Aware Adaptive Scheduler Generation for Linux

Authors: Yifan Zhang, Hengxuan Lou, Xinkui Zhao, Rengrong Xiong, Naibo Wang, Chang Liu, Guanjie Cheng, Shuiguang Deng, Jianwei Yin

Abstract: Modern Linux scheduling increasingly requires workload-specific policies to meet the dynamic performance needs of heterogeneous applications. The recent sched_ext framework enables custom BPF schedulers, but building practical policies still requires coordinating Rust user-space control logic, BPF scheduling callbacks, generated user-kernel interfaces, and strict eBPF verifier constraints. This paper presents MorphSched, an LLM-driven system for synthesizing complete Rust+BPF sched_ext schedulers. MorphSched profiles target workloads under Linux EEVDF to construct dynamic, workload-specific optimization objectives, and uses a scaffold-constrained generation pipeline to translate scheduling intent into deployable scheduler implementations. To improve synthesis reliability, MorphSched separates implementation repair from strategy search: verifier, build, and runtime failures are handled through a DAG-structured repair process, while performance feedback is accumulated in an evolutionary memory pool to refine high-level scheduling strategies across iterations. We evaluate MorphSched across 120 machine/workload/interferer configurations on a real Linux kernel. The generated schedulers compile, pass eBPF verification, load successfully, and remain stable throughout our benchmark runs without manual edits to generated scheduler source code. Across these configurations, MorphSched improves the primary foreground metric over EEVDF by 26.5% on average and up to 87.3%. In selected comparisons, the generated schedulers are also competitive with representative hand-written sched_ext schedulers. These results show that scaffold-constrained, system-grounded LLM generation can be a practical tool for exploring workload-specific Linux scheduling policies.

cross Subliminal Learning in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Arman Hatami, Romina Aalishah, Ilya E. Monosov

Abstract: Subliminal learning is a phenomenon in large language models (LLMs) where a teacher passes a hidden preference to a student through training data unrelated to that preference. We study whether subliminal learning also occurs in vision-language models (VLMs). We finetune a teacher VLM to prefer a specific animal or facial attribute and use a control teacher from the same base model. Both teachers generate numbers for an unrelated visual task involving geometric shapes. Two students initialized from the same base VLM are finetuned on the same images, using numbers generated by either the biased or control teacher. Across VLMs ranging from 2B to 7B parameters, we find that both animal and facial preferences can transfer through this unrelated task. Students trained on biased-teacher outputs select the target more often than control students, with a difference of up to $\sim$10 percentage points in the animal preference task. Transfer can become stronger with more training data and varies with the target preference, model, and how the numbers are generated. Beyond preferences, we show that the biased student's explanations use more of the same words and phrases as the biased teacher's explanations than those of the control student, even when all three models select the same image. These results matter for training VLMs and show that training on unrelated data may change both a VLM's image preferences and how it explains its choices, and that transfer can become stronger with more training samples.

cross SAFTAC: Simulation-Augmented Fine-Tuning of Open-Source LLMs for Analog Circuit Design

Authors: Junsheng Huang, Yifan Sun, Zhuoer Zhang, Ning Wei, Yening Liu, Michael Molter, Pavan Kumar Hanumolu, Elyse Rosenbaum, Bin Hu, Huan Zhang

Abstract: Circuit design for analog integrated circuits (ICs) is challenging because circuit topology, device sizing, and circuit performance are tightly coupled. Existing large language model (LLM)-based methods either rely on inference-time correction around frozen models or train on limited circuit types without directly using simulation outcomes as training signals. A key bottleneck is that existing datasets do not provide sufficient task information for training LLMs on end-to-end specification-conditioned design. To fill this gap, we first construct a large-scale simulation-grounded dataset with 8,626 design tasks across 10 different types of analog circuits, where each task includes a textual description, labeled I/O ports, loading condition, target specifications, a testbench, and a reference netlist. Using this dataset, we present SAFTAC, a simulation-augmented fine-tuning framework that trains LLMs to generate and revise analog circuit design. SAFTAC combines three-stage supervised fine-tuning, which progressively builds the capabilities required for analog circuit design and feedback-guided correction, with simulation feedback-augmented reinforcement fine-tuning, which further optimizes models using simulation-grounded rewards. We evaluate SAFTAC under single-pass generation and two-pass generation with simulation feedback, showing improvements over the strongest closed-source LLM by 15.9% and 7.8%, respectively.

cross PINNeval: A Comprehensive Evaluation Standard for Physics-Informed Neural Networks

Authors: Kevin von Bargen, Hamid Ebrahimy, Tim R\"omer

Abstract: The Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) literature has produced a rapidly expanding catalogue of methods. We divide a PINN training pipeline systematically into four components and observe that many existing benchmarks study these components in isolation. These components are domain-importance adaptation, loss balancing, neural network architecture, and optimization scheme. Moreover, the studies set the surrounding and not considered components of the pipeline to a minimal default that is no longer representative of current best practice approaches. Conclusions drawn under such conditions may not transfer to competitive pipelines. In this work, we propose a new evaluation protocol that (1) treats the four components as an integrated system; (2) crucially requires competitive implementations of the components not under investigation; (3) specifies the metrics and reporting format; and (4) is accompanied by structured documentation that ensures the traceability of evaluations across studies. Based on this comprehensive protocol, we release PINNpipe, an innovative JAX package containing 18 different methods from the current literature as well as nine efficiently implemented benchmark problems. Using PINNpipe, we illustrate our evaluation protocol in relation to established benchmark studies and report results that challenge common assumptions.

Authors: Hengwei Zhao, Qirui Wang, Zhuo Zheng, Xiaoyan Lu, Rusty Feagin, Wenzhe Jiao

Abstract: Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) are reshaping remote sensing by shifting the field from task-specific modeling toward general-purpose geospatial intelligence. Mapping the damage to buildings stands to benefit substantially from this shift, as GFMs pretrained on large-scale Earth observation data provide generalizable geospatial representations that can facilitate effective responses to sudden-onset disasters. Although parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) facilitates GFM adaptation, parameter efficiency does not necessarily translate into label efficiency. Building damage mapping remains challenging due to scarce pre-existing labeled data or limited time for annotation. When weak supervision is introduced to alleviate the labeling bottleneck, we identify the difficulty of learning sufficiently discriminative representations as a key challenge for label-efficient GFM adaptation. To address this challenge, we propose a Positive-Unlabeled GFM Adaptation (PUMA) framework that requires annotations for only a small number of damaged buildings. More specifically, PUMA integrates non-contrastive weakly supervised representation refinement to mitigate the impact of noisy supervision on representation learning from a gradient perspective, and latent-state-based label disambiguation to enable reversible supervision refinement without requiring class prior estimation. Extensive experiments on disaster benchmark datasets from around the world demonstrated the effectiveness and robustness of PUMA. A real-world application to the 2025 Los Angeles wildfires further highlighted its potential to accurately map damage under highly limited supervision. More broadly, these findings suggest that weakly supervised PEFT offers a label-efficient pathway from GFMs to real-world applications.

cross CascadeRec: Deployed Adaptive LLM Compute Allocation for Multi-Tenant Content Recommendation

Authors: Kai Zhao, Guilin Zhang, Xiang Cheng, Xiquan Cui, Henry Heng, Xu Chu, Alet Blanken

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are attractive rerankers for recommendation. Running an expensive model on every request is costly, and for users with sparse interaction histories it often lowers quality. We present CascadeRec, a two-tier LLM reranking cascade deployed in production at a large financial planning and analysis (FP&A) SaaS provider. It serves content recommendations to enterprise tenants on AWS EKS and GKE. A lightweight Tier-1 model reranks every request. A router escalates only the requests where extra inference-time compute is likely to help, and sends them to a more expensive Tier-2 procedure (self-consistency sampling, multi-path reasoning, or a larger model). The system has run in a production A/B experiment for over a month. Compared with uniform use of the expensive tier, CascadeRec has reduced inference cost by about 10%, with minimal latency impact and no quality regression observed to date. Tenant data cannot be released, so we also evaluate the pipeline on public benchmarks (MovieLens-1M and Amazon-Electronics). Segment-level analysis with paired-bootstrap confidence intervals shows that uniform LLM reranking lowers NDCG@10 for sparse-history users by up to 30%, and its only statistically significant gain appears on the segment with the longest histories. Escalating cold users can make the loss larger instead of repairing it. Which segments benefit from escalation depends on the domain, and this motivates the segment-gated rollout we use in production. We describe the deployment architecture, the measured benefits, and lessons learned, including inter-tenant load imbalance as a driver for cascading and the handling of LLM output-parsing failures at the serving layer.

cross Q-OIL: Optimistic Exploration in Learning from Human Interventions

Authors: Pranav Teegavarapu, Sriyash Poddar, Rico Qi, Cleah Winston, Krisha Khandelwal, Aykut Onyol, Natasha Jaques, Abhishek Gupta

Abstract: As robots are deployed in the real world, they will inevitably make mistakes. Human interventions provide valuable feedback for improving their behavior, enabling them to adapt online to new scenarios. However, studying human-in-the-loop robot learning is difficult: real-world experiments are slow and expensive, so most algorithm development relies on simulated human models. We show that common human modeling assumptions do not match how real people intervene in practice. Through analyzing intervention data from human participants, we find that humans tend to intervene when agent progress stalls, and that their corrective actions are often suboptimal. Motivated by these findings, we introduce a progress-based model of human intervention that achieves higher rank correlation with real-user algorithm rankings, enabling realistic algorithm benchmarking and development in simulation. Building on these insights, we propose Q-OIL, a method that adds optimistic bonuses to intervention transitions in reinforcement learning and softly guides exploration toward human corrections. We find that decoupled critics are critical for stability because they prevent the bonuses from backing up to pre-intervention states. As a result, Q-OIL learns more efficiently than competitive baselines, achieving 10-50% higher success in simulation and on real robots, and improving pretrained VLAs on two bimanual manipulation tasks. As we ground both evaluation and algorithms in realistic human behavior, this work provides a practical path toward scalable robot learning from interventions.

cross HaNDF: Object-Conditioned Geometric Neural Hand Distance Fields

Authors: Zuhao Liu, Melih Darcan, Zhengdi Yu, Riza Alp Guler, Tolga Birdal

Abstract: We propose, HaNDF, object-conditional neural distance fields as data-driven priors for modeling the plausible hand-object configurations, in the product manifold of hand articulations. While recent unconditional generative priors showed success in modeling human pose, hands are versatile articulated bodies often found in interaction with other objects. This makes it crucial to develop a generative prior which can model the plausible hand poses conditioned on the object under interaction. To this end, our HaNDF proposes a geometry-aware, object-conditioned neural distance field, representing the plausible articulations in the zero-level set of a conditional field. We leverage tools from Riemannian geometry to (i) represent plausible hands in the zero level-set of an object-induced neural field, and (ii) project a given pose onto this field. Using HaNDF, we can also optimize for a plausible condition, determining the object under interaction. Our extensive evaluations demonstrate that our HaNDF provides a flexible and powerful prior for hand-object interaction, suitable for applications such as pose refinement, reconstruction under occlusion, and physically plausible manipulation synthesis.

Abstract: Groups can achieve more than individuals in the same amount of time, but only if their members coordinate: they must decide who works on what and what to share. We ask whether agents can instead learn to organize themselves. Rather than being designed in advance, s$^3$ enables organization to emerge from a shared goal. This opens the door to capable systems built from many models that learn to work together.

Authors: Zanting Ye, Yunrui Lin, Shengyuan Liu, Jing Hao, Chen Hu, Yefeng Zheng, Lijun Lu

Abstract: Medical vision--language models can interpret volumetric images, but processing full volumes is costly because hundreds of slices produce long, redundant visual-token sequences. Existing compression methods often couple content selection with budget determination. We observe that neighboring slices share visual features and that attention concentration varies across visual encoders. We propose VoluCompose, a training-free, model-agnostic framework that separates what visual content to retain from how many tokens to allocate. Its hyperbolic token composition module groups contiguous slices into ordered support units and uses the Lorentz distance to account for feature magnitude and direction during token allocation, anchor selection, and refinement. Its capacity control module uses visual-encoder attention entropy to allocate more tokens when attention is more dispersed. We evaluate VoluCompose on two tasks across three datasets and four backbones. Across nine calibrated settings, VoluCompose attains the highest mean quality--retention hypervolume among the compared methods. It achieves a mean relative quality of 121.68\% against the uncompressed references while retaining 22.78% of visual tokens on average. Compression remains robust even at a 10% token budget. Across settings, the lowest ROUGE-L ratio to the uncompressed reference is 96.4\% for VoluCompose. The corresponding minima for the compression baselines range from 15.0% to 70.5%. Code is available at https://github.com/yezanting/VoluCompose.

Authors: Laya Iyer, Sanmi Koyejo, Chen Shani

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been evaluated on their ability to identify emotions expressed in text and produce fluent emotional language. cross Dual-Stream LLM-Guided Reward Design for Reject-Inference-Aware Reinforcement Learning in E-Commerce Payment Fraud Detection

Authors: Bo Qu, Zhurong Wang, Frank Zahradnik, Ming Ouyang

Abstract: Production fraud-detection models learn from a censored slice of their own traffic: transactions a legacy system blocked never receive fraud labels, so models train on a biased, approved-only distribution. We extend LLM-guided reward design, the framework of Qu et al. (2025) in which an LLM evolves the reward of a two-step REINFORCE fraud-blocking policy, to attack this reject-inference problem inside the reward. A single LLM designs a dual-stream reward: stream 1 applies the standard dollar-weighted precision objective to labeled transactions; stream 2 scores legacy-blocked, unlabeled transactions by contrasting the newest gradient-boosted risk signal against the legacy scores that drove the original block. Rather than prescribe which signals matter, we expose a superset of evidence and let the LLM decide for itself which to use, guided by evaluation feedback alone; because it writes the reward as code, that choice is directly auditable. On a held-out four-month out-of-time test, the dual-stream agent beats the prior labeled-only agent at every target recall, by +4.7, +2.4 and +3.0 points of dollar-weighted precision at 80%, 85% and 90% recall, and the production baseline by +6.8 points on average. The resulting policy is architecturally identical to that of Qu et al. (2025) and adds no inference cost.

cross An Educated Guess: Deriving Statistically Aligned Gradient Estimates for Zeroth-Order Optimisation

Authors: Justus Huebotter, Serge Thill, Marcel van Gerven, Nasir Ahmad

Abstract: As novel hardware architectures are explored for the purposes of machine learning, including neuromorphic chips, asynchronous systems, and ASICs, effective learning algorithms are highly desirable. While backpropagation is the standard for differentiation on standard hardware, its requirement for global synchrony and exact differentiability limits the exploration of exotic algorithms on non-traditional substrates and devices. Zeroth-order (ZO) optimisation methods, such as SPSA and weight perturbation, offer a compelling alternative by estimating gradients through inference-only passes; however, these methods have historically suffered due to the `curse of dimensionality', where random perturbations become increasingly misaligned with the true gradient in high-dimensional spaces. In this work, we move beyond blind random noise by deriving zeroth-order search directions from activation geometry available during the forward pass. This leads to a family of Spectral Zeroth-Order methods, SZO-$\kappa$, whose directions solve local response-based gradient-guessing objectives under explicit stochastic assumptions. These methods explore feature-supported directions that are more likely to produce informative loss responses. Using multilayer and convolutional networks, we analyse gradient alignment, bias-variance structure, and training stability of these methods relative to standard perturbation baselines and backpropagation.

cross Patching the Blind Spots: Reversible LLM Rule Overlay for Frozen Fraud Detectors

Authors: Bo Qu, Zhurong Wang, Frank Zahradnik, Ming Ouyang

Abstract: In industrial tabular fraud classification, gradient-boosted trees remain state-of-the-art, but even a well-trained model has blind spots, and retraining the deployed model is not always safe. We propose a framework that patches such a model without modifying it: LLMs propose rules anchored on features the model underweights by SHAP, and the resulting noisy pool is screened for empirical support and calibrated into an additive, reversible log-lift overlay that runs at the production cut-off. cross Remembering Is Not Enough: Evaluating Timing and Omission in Longitudinal AI Mental Health Support

Authors: Eli Min, Mohit Chandra, Aamogh Sawant, Mengting Tu, Freya Lin, Ridwan Shams Khan, Reigna El-Yashruti, Christopher L. Smith, Mark Kalinich, John Torous, Munmun De Choudhury

Abstract: Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support commonly focus on the clinical appropriateness of individual responses, potentially overlooking failures that emerge across extended interactions. As AI assistants accumulate memory of users' circumstances and prior disclosures, this raises an accountability question: Do AI chatbots provide appropriate support as users' needs evolve? We investigate by analyzing 14,256 longitudinal simulations grounded in donated chatbot histories from 22 users, examining how a memory-equipped assistant responds to suicidal ideation, self-harm, and anxiety crises across recovery trajectories and communication styles. Although explicitly unproductive advice was rare, the assistant frequently omitted clinician-expected support such as follow-up and planning for next steps. These findings show the limits of single response-level safety evaluation and the need to assess timing, omission, and consistency across users when holding AI systems accountable for longitudinal support.

cross Random Symbolic Boosting: A Geometric Approach to Symbolic Regression

Authors: Jos\'e R. Ortegaa, Jos\'e Manuel Mu\~noz Contreras, Leonardo Trujillo

Abstract: Symbolic models offer interpretability, but their construction through generational evolution can be computationally expensive. This article presents Random Symbolic Boosting (RSB), a method that constructs explicit additive symbolic models via boosting over populations of randomly generated syntax trees, eliminating the need for generational evolution. The idea combines two observations: high-dimensional spaces can be spanned by enough linearly independent vectors, and the semantics of syntax trees can be directly manipulated as such vectors. It is shown that, for Ordinary Least Squares, the boosting process guarantees a monotonic reduction of the training error for both regression and classification. Furthermore, an adaptive geometric early stopping mechanism is introduced. It is based on the angle between the prediction and target vectors, is independent of the problems scale, and requires no adjustments across tasks. Evaluated on a standard benchmark for symbolic regression, the method is located at the Pareto-optimal point between predictive accuracy and training time, ranking first on the real-world datasets. Additionally, it was tested on a large tabular classification benchmark with 200 problems, achieving competitive results.

cross AtariBench: Evaluating Spatiotemporal Reasoning of Vision-Language Models in Atari Gameplay

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on static visual understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to act in dynamic environments remains less well understood. We introduce AtariBench, a controlled benchmark that evaluates VLMs across 20 Atari 2600 games, requiring models to make sequential decisions from high-temporal-resolution gameplay streams. Overall, AtariBench exposes a persistent gap between passive visual perception and active decision-making, providing a lightweight and rigorous testbed for developing VLMs that can reason and act in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Goals are central to how people, teams, and organizations structure their work. cross CrowdDirector: Runtime Orchestration of Crowd Simulation through Natural-Language Directives

Authors: Xiaohan Sun, Vuk Radovanovic, Merce Alvarez, Carol O'Sullivan, Yiorgos Chrysanthou, Panayiotis Charalambous

Abstract: Natural language offers an intuitive way to direct a virtual crowd while a simulation is running. Handwritten rules execute efficiently but cannot respond flexibly to varied instructions, whereas asking a large language model (LLM) to control the crowd at every update introduces repeated latency, cost, and the risk of unsupported actions. We present CrowdDirector, a framework that uses an LLM only when the user adds or changes an instruction. The LLM translates the request into a structured instruction, and a scene validator checks whether the entities and actions it references exist in the current Unity scene. Accepted instructions are stored and remain continuously active as the simulation is running. At each simulation tick, a graph neural network uses the current scene state and active instruction to choose an available action and target for each agent, while Unity handles movement, interactions, and animation. The network is trained offline on simulated scenarios, with LLM guidance on how agents should respond to instructions. Our evaluations show the scene validator's feasibility decisions matched human judgements more often than the LLM's own assessment, and on a behavioural benchmark CrowdDirector follows user instructions more closely than handwritten rules while avoiding repeated LLM calls. It therefore provides a practical middle ground between fixed rules and continuous LLM control.

cross Conflux: Preventing Privilege Escalation with Principal Contexts and System-Level Evaluation

Authors: Raya Buckley, Christian Schroeder de Witt

Abstract: LLM agents combine information from multiple principals while acting externally. We formalise privilege escalation (PE) as the system-level security objective: an action may proceed only if every principal that influenced it would allow it. Model-level defences remain probabilistic, so enforcement must sit outside model judgement. We present Conflux, a principal-context defence. Its kernel, ITES, derives authority from provenance and the organisation's existing access-control system and intersects the permissions of every influencing principal. We prove a conditional authority-confinement theorem: under complete mediation, no-laundering provenance, a known non-empty decision context, fresh permission checks, and action binding, the kernel admits no action that escalates privilege with respect to any recorded influencing principal. The theorem covers the represented value-influence fragment; control influence from tool, branch, recipient, or plan-step selection remains a refinement obligation. Conflux extends the kernel with independent policy ports, argument-aware authorisation, disclosure, consent, delegation, restriction, and exposure planning. We describe two system- level evaluators. SLED is a black-box evaluator: it exhaustively explores a declared finite model of a defence up to a bound. SLED-V is a white-box evaluator: it checks a serialisable intermediate representation against security and provenance properties. The evidence is bounded and partial. The appendix reports retained AgentDojo, SLED, and SLED-V results, including the limits of those runs. No result in this paper is an unbounded deployment guarantee.

cross Federated training performs as well as centralized for 3D Latent Diffusion for Diagnosis-Conditioned Brain MRI Synthesis

Abstract: Generative models of brain MRI are useful to understand brain conditions, and to enrich scarce and imbalanced neuroimaging data. cross On Lipschitz Explosion in Deep Neural Networks with Normalization: Consequences for Optimization and Adversarial Robustness

Authors: Ashkan Soleymani, Reyhaneh Hosseinpourkhoshkbari, Hadi Daneshmand, Patrick Jaillet

Abstract: Can a network look stable at every layer and still be exponentially unstable as a whole? The Lipschitz constant of a neural network is a basic stability quantity appearing in optimization guarantees, generalization bounds, and robustness certificates. Deep networks naturally admit two such notions, input Lipschitzness, measuring sensitivity to data perturbations, and parameter Lipschitzness, measuring sensitivity to weight perturbations. We prove that, for deep networks with normalization layers, with batch normalization as the canonical example, both Lipschitz constants can grow exponentially with depth and hence with parameter dimension, even under strong per-layer norm control such as $\|W_\ell\|_2\le 1$, and already for linear activations. Thus, a mechanism widely used to stabilize training can create severe worst-case instabilities that are invisible locally, i.e., from layerwise norm bounds alone. Parameter-Lipschitz explosion turns Lipschitz-dependent nonsmooth optimization guarantees into exponential-in-dimension bounds for deep normalized networks, while input-Lipschitz explosion makes worst-case Lipschitz-based generalization and robustness certificates vacuous. The latter also yields a concrete rank-separation mechanism for adversarial vulnerability. The theory predicts that perturbations introducing new singular directions should be amplified much more strongly than perturbations at the same noise level that remain within the input's original singular subspace. Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 support this prediction, showing that rank-creating perturbations cause substantially sharper drops in accuracy, confidence, and margins than same-subspace perturbations.

Authors: Stephen Peng, Vineet Jagadeesan Nair, Noah Broestl, Anne Friederike Kleppe, Steven Mills, Adi Zolotov

Abstract: The number of domain fine-tuned models, particularly in the biomedical domain, is rapidly expanding. Fine-tuning promises high-performing models at a fraction of the cost of training a base model, but it can also produce unintended behavioral changes. Multiple studies have shown that safety guardrails can erode with fine-tuning, yet large-scale studies across model architectures, data mixtures, and fine-tuning methodologies remain limited. Where prior work in this space has studied value-centric refusal, this work focuses on knowledge-centric abstention. We audit 25 biomedical domain fine-tuned large language models against 11 paired base checkpoints on MedAbstain and ClinDet-Bench to evaluate the impact of fine-tuning on knowledge-centric abstention. We find that defined appropriate abstention measured by these benchmarks, already low in base models (median 8.3% on MedAbstain), falls further after fine-tuning (median 4.2%, decreasing in 21 of 25 pairs on MedAbstain), and that the magnitude and direction of change are heterogeneous across pairs. These results motivate fine-tuning methods that improve domain-specific capability while preserving appropriate caution.

cross Learning from How Users Interpret: Personalizing Recommendation Explanation Order

Authors: Deo Munduku, Elsa Negre

Abstract: Personalized explanations typically adapt their content or presentation based on preferences, interaction histories, or other user characteristics. We examine whether regularities inferred from prior textual interpretations can support personalization of explanation organization. In a controlled within participant study with 122 participants, prior interpretations were represented along three dimensions: information selection, relative salience, and relational organization. The relational dimension was partially operationalized through co mobilization. cross Training Stability under Adaptive Curricula

Authors: Johan Obando-Ceron, Walter Mayor, Michael Dennis, Aaron Courville, Pablo Samuel Castro

Abstract: Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approaches train reinforcement learning (RL) agents on diverse curricula that may evolve with their capabilities. While prior work has largely focused on how these curricula are constructed, we study whether their students remain trainable under the resulting distributions. Across multiple UED mechanisms, environment complexities, and extended training horizons, we identify recurring learner-side pathologies, including parameter growth, degraded value--outcome ordering, and loss of representational diversity, that accompany stagnation and performance degradation. These findings connect limitations in UED to optimization failures previously identified in conventional deep RL, while showing how adaptive curricula couple the learner's optimization state to its future training distribution. Controlled interventions show that mitigating selected pathologies improves learning. Our results establish learner trainability as a complementary design principle for UED. Effective curricula require students that remain capable of learning from the distributions they produce.

cross Not All Latents Are Equal: Importance-Weighted Latent Graph Generation

Authors: Biraaj Rout, Jay Revolinsky, Han Liu, Harry Shomer

Abstract: Graph generation has shown tremendous effectiveness in modeling complex scientific tasks ranging from molecule generation to optimized circuit design. Most recent advancements have developed models that learn to denoise noisy graphs using either discrete diffusion or flow matching. However, discrete graph diffusion scales quadratically with the number of nodes, making it difficult to use such models on larger graphs. On the other hand, latent graph generation instead seeks to denoise noisy graph latents, which scale better to more nodes due to their lower dimension. However, in practice, latent graph generation models lag behind their discrete counterparts in performance. We study this problem, finding that it is caused by the unequal distribution of that different latent dimensions have on graph reconstruction. the impact. We observe that the vast majority of impact is contained in only a smaller number of latent dimensions. Since equal emphasis is placed on reconstructing the entire latent in the loss function, existing models are optimized to reconstruct information irrelevant to the downstream task. To tackle this problem, we propose Latent Importance Reweighting ({\bf LIR)} which weights each latent dimensions via it's importance to final graph reconstruction. Importance is calculated as the relative influence of a single latent unit on graph reconstruction, which is represented via the Jacobian of the decoder relative to the latent. Our method is model-agnostic and requires no additional model parameters or hyperparameters. Experiments show that LIR can boost performance and lead to faster training convergence when applied to two different autoencoders on various benchmark datasets. Code: https://github.com/biraaj/GraphGenLatentReweight

URLs: https://github.com/biraaj/GraphGenLatentReweight

cross On the Incompleteness of do-calculus for Universal Identifiability

Authors: Yaroslav Kivva, Aliaksandra Novik, Negar Kiyavash

Abstract: We study the problem of causal effect identification from an arbitrary set of observational and/or interventional distributions, which we call Universal Identifiability (uID). The uID problem generalizes existing classical identifiability (ID) and general identifiability (gID) problems, which considered the identification problem from either mere observational distributions or a specific type of interventional distributions. Prior research has established graphical criteria to determine when a causal effect is identifiable in ID and gID problems. Moreover, algorithms that leverage do-calculus and basic probability manipulations have been proposed to compute these effects. Consequently, it has been shown that do-calculus is both sound and complete in these problems. Despite these well-established results, a formal definition of what it means for the do-calculus to be complete does not exist. We propose a rigorous formalization for the completeness of the do-calculus and show that the completeness results in previous work remain valid. However, for more general problems, such as uID, the rules of do-calculus are no longer complete. This is perhaps the reason why the uID problem has remained open thus far.

cross Ordered Policy Optimization

Authors: Dongkyu Derek Cho, Pan Xu

Abstract: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) stabilizes policy updates by clipping the importance ratio between the new policy and the data-collecting policy. The standard rule uses one fixed clipping range for all samples. While this choice offers simplicity and effectiveness, it fails to distinguish statistically harmful extreme ratio values from large, yet tolerable changes. We study how to adapt the clipping radius to the reliability of the induced importance ratio distribution. Our case study demonstrates that reliable updates require controlling these extreme ratios, rather than merely verifying that individual ratios fall within a fixed interval. Motivated by this observation, we propose Ordered Policy Optimization (\textsc{OPO}). In each minibatch, \textsc{OPO} sorts the importance ratios, selects the extreme ratios that potentially have the largest effect on the update, and assigns them adaptive sample-wise radii instead of using the fixed clipping radius. These radii are derived from a prescribed tail profile, which specifies desired target values for the selected extreme ratios. The resulting objective remains first-order and requires only minibatch sorting and a modified policy loss. Experiments conducted on MuJoCo and Atari benchmarks demonstrate that this simple modification achieves strong performance relative to established baselines.

Authors: Jianuo Shen, Pengyi Xu, Bo Zhang

Abstract: Translating parallel scientific codes between HPC programming frameworks is a critical but labor-intensive task. Authors: Disha Sheshanarayana, Rajat Subhra Pal, Manjira Sinha, Tirthankar Dasgupta

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly aggregate judgments across model instances, but reasoning about other agents can itself introduce failure. We call this failure phantom consensus and study it as a pluralistic-ignorance-style pattern in controlled LLM collectives. Authors: Colin Priest

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to resolve consumer complaints, and the way complainants write varies with age, education and socioeconomic status (SES). We audit three remedy-decision systems using sociolinguistic rewrites of US consumer financial complaints that are instructed to preserve every fact: two generative LLMs that return a single label (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-5-mini) and a probabilistic "System One" model that returns a distribution over remedies (Jev). Using pre-registered hypotheses tested once on held-out complaints, we separate two phenomena that single-label audits conflate. At the group scale, older, college-educated and higher-SES styles receive slightly more generous remedies; these effects replicate but are small, with every monetary-remedy gap within 0.5 percentage points. At the individual scale, two style versions of the same complaint receive different monetary decisions 2.6-6.3% of the time. In a variance-component analysis of the probabilistic model, persona components shared across the two rewrite models account for about 1.6% of within-complaint variation. The persona gaps are associated mainly with delivery (vocabulary, grammar, politeness) rather than content. Positive controls show that all three systems respond 49-387 times more strongly to remedy-relevant facts than to style. Rewriting every complaint into one standard style reduced GPT-4o-mini's education and SES gaps by 94% and 87% while retaining fact sensitivity, but reversed two of the probabilistic model's gaps and did not make individual decisions more stable. Independent LLM auditors flagged material fact changes in 3.3% and 11.7% of rewrites, and instability persisted among unflagged rewrites. Fairness audits of LLM decision systems should report group disparities and individual instability as separate outcomes.

cross Seeing vs. Counting: Complexity Twins Show Where Multimodal LLMs Fail on Diagrams

Authors: Yin Liu

Abstract: When a multimodal LLM miscounts in a diagram, does it fail at seeing or at counting? Existing visual graph and puzzle benchmarks vary visual density and algorithmic hardness together, confounding the two. We pair counting questions about the same drawing that sit in different counting-complexity classes, such as its spanning trees (a determinant) and its simple paths (#P-complete), and use a transcription probe and a text-only control to separate reading from computing. We test Gemini, Gemma and Qwen3-VL models; for the Gemini models, the answer depends on how the model computes. In chain of thought, most of them read small drawings correctly and then fail at counting, at a rate set far more by the size of the answer than by its complexity class (for the primary model, the odds of an exact answer fall 2.38-fold per decade of the answer). Given code and an exact transcription, the primary model counts correctly even on 10x10 grids, and reading becomes the bottleneck: a single view misses horizontal edges, and a second reading of a rotated copy with crop checks reads 86/96 large grids exactly, against 57/96 when the upright reading is simply repeated.

cross Capital Efficiency, Isolation, Risk, and Ruin in Generative AI Inference Serving Infrastructure

Authors: Ren\'ee St. Amant, Anjaly Parayil, Ankur Mallick, Victor R\"uhle

Abstract: Generative AI serving is an extremely capital-intensive endeavor that demands rapid, step-change improvements in capital efficiency to prevent industry wide increases in the cost of capital from limiting growth plans. Keeping pace with the economic necessities will increasingly require infrastructure providers to confront operationally-inconvenient realities of production serving, including the extreme heterogeneity of generative AI request costs. We have explored workload isolation as a foundational, fleet-level lever for increasing capital efficiency. Isolation is the practice of intelligently grouping "like" requests before execution. Rather than exposing every replica to the full heterogeneous workload distribution, isolation deliberately reshapes the workload presented to the hardware by assigning requests with different cost characteristics to different portions of the fleet. Workload isolation on its own does not make expensive requests cheaper; it prevents a costly portion of the workload from unnecessarily determining the operating point and capital efficiency of the entire fleet. It additionally lays the foundational groundwork that can serve as a launching point for compounding gains via narrow-band optimizations (tailoring) and custom silicon designs while also informing up on pricing policy. This paper uses representative production data and experience to convey the motivation behind isolation as a fleet-level lever for increased capital efficiency.

cross Did I See It or Think It? Probing Reality Monitoring in Vision-Language Models with Concept Injection

Authors: Tina Khezresmaeilzadeh, Seyedarmin Azizi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram

Abstract: Recent work tests whether language models can introspect by injecting a concept direction into their activations and asking whether they notice it and can name it. These studies are text-only. Vision-language models (VLMs) face an additional problem, known in cognitive psychology as reality monitoring: they must keep internally generated content separate from what they actually see. We therefore ask: can VLMs distinguish injected concepts from image content? We introduce a paradigm that varies the image and the injected concept independently. On each trial, the model reports what it sees, whether a thought was injected, and what that thought is. This design scores both whether the model identifies the concept and which source it assigns to it. The same failure appears in ordinary captioning, where injected objects appear in up to 87% of captions of real photographs. Evaluating multimodal self-reports therefore requires checking both their content and their reported source.

cross Optimal Probabilistic Answer Set Programming: Complexity and Implementations

Authors: Damiano Azzolini, Giuseppe Mazzotta, Francesco Ricca, Fabrizio Riguzzi

Abstract: Probabilistic Answer Set Programming (PASP) extends Answer Set Programming with the possibility of representing uncertain data. Among the possible available semantics, we focus here on the credal semantics. In this setting, prior work has focused exclusively on plain ASP programs, i.e., programs without optimization constructs. However, many real world tasks define optimality criteria over the possible solutions, which cannot be conveniently modeled with existing probabilistic ASP frameworks. In this paper, we close this gap and propose optimal Probabilistic Answer Set Programming, which extends PASP by also allowing weak constraints, which are the standard optimization construct of ASP. We define the semantics of such programs, study the computational complexity of the inference and most probable explanation tasks, and propose practical implementations based on Algebraic Model Counting and Answer Set Programming with Quantifiers. An empirical evaluation of the proposed approach on three real world tasks confirms the applicability of our framework.

Abstract: Agentic robotics asks coding agents to implement and iteratively improve robot policies from simulated and real-world physical interactions. cross Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection for Reasoning Distillation

Authors: Yijun Zhu, Jianxin Wang, Chengchao Shen

Abstract: Training smaller language models on reasoning solutions generated by a larger teacher model is the prevailing approach for transferring mathematical reasoning capabilities. However, the student tends to overfit the output distribution of the teacher and generalizes poorly when its own behavior at inference time deviates from the training data. Recent methods attempt to bridge this gap by incorporating error data, yet the corrective trajectories still reflect the reasoning patterns of the teacher, which the student cannot reliably reproduce, leaving the distribution mismatch largely unresolved. We propose a method, Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection (FPRT), built on the insight that effective training data should be jointly determined by what the student fails on and what it can realistically learn. Our method first profiles the failure behavior of the student on each problem through repeated sampling, characterizing it in terms of error rate and error consistency to reveal qualitatively distinct failure modes. Each mode demands a fundamentally different training signal. The diagnosed mode then guides candidate generation from the teacher via distinct prompting strategies tailored to each failure type. Among the correct candidates, our method selects the one whose initial reasoning steps after the point of divergence are most reproducible by the student. These first corrective steps constitute the primary bottleneck, while the remaining steps follow an already corrected context and can therefore be continued more readily. This contrasts with standard reranking approaches, which score the trajectory as a whole and thereby dilute the signal at the critical reasoning transition. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves the SOTA performance across the five benchmarks on both student backbones.

cross Equidistribution of Solution Gradients in Physics-Informed Neural Networks via Coordinate Transformations

Authors: Tianchi Yu, Ivan Oseledets

Abstract: Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) often struggle to approximate solutions with sharp gradients due to optimization difficulties and spectral bias. Motivated by the principle of gradient equidistribution from the stretching approach in grid generation, which redistributes solution gradients to reduce functional complexity, we propose Coordinate Transformation PINNs (CTPINNs), which incorporate learnable coordinate transformations to map the physical domain into a computational domain where the physical solutions become approximately affine, making them easier for neural networks to learn. We propose two constructions of the transformation: an explicit construction based on Majorants and an implicit construction based on invertable neural networks. Experimentally, the explicit construction can improve accuracy for PDEs with available explicit coordinate transformations, and the implicit construction can be combined with other advanced PINN models as an efficient plug-in module to improve performance across a range of challenging PDE benchmarks.

cross UniRGBA: Unifying RGB and Alpha in Pretrained Video Diffusion Models

Abstract: Recent advances in video diffusion models have achieved remarkable photorealistic RGB synthesis, yet generating videos with a native alpha channel remains an open challenge. cross GlobalPuzzle: Globally Informed Genetic Search for Eroded Jigsaw Puzzle Reassembly

Authors: Lunyan Wang, Chuanzhi Xu, Ziyuan Tao, Weidong Cai

Abstract: Jigsaw puzzle reassembly remains a highly challenging task, particularly when piece boundaries are irregularly eroded to simulate damage to cultural artifacts. On the GAP benchmark, existing methods achieve a perfect assembly accuracy of only 0.3% on $5 \times 5$ puzzles, whereas humans can solve jigsaw puzzles with remarkably high accuracy. Humans typically approach puzzle solving by identifying potentially adjacent pieces, evaluating the current assembly, and iteratively performing complex operations such as adding pieces, replacing incorrectly placed pieces, and moving entire connected groups of pieces. However, existing methods face a fundamental limitation: approaches that leverage global visual information typically predict piece arrangements directly, making it difficult to explicitly perform such complex iterative operations, while search based methods can rearrange pieces but primarily rely on pairwise boundary compatibility, lacking global visual information to evaluate and guide the search process. cross Training-Free Active Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language Models

Authors: Jihwan Bang, Sumyeong Ahn, Hwanjun Song, Jae-Gil Lee

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) for vision-language models (VLMs) is usually studied in a fully unsupervised regime, where adaptation relies on pseudo-labels, confidence scores, or entropy computed by the model itself. This is a severe restriction on hard shifted samples: the model is asked to repair its own uncertain predictions without any external evidence. At the other extreme, supervised online adaptation assumes labels can directly train or correct the model. We study the intermediate deployment regime between these two extremes. In Active Test-time Adaptation for VLMs (Active VLM-TTA), a pretrained VLM may spend an explicit labeling budget on selected test samples, but the prediction for the current sample must be emitted before its label can be used; queried labels are therefore costed, delayed evidence for future samples rather than free correction for the present one. Under this future-only constraint, the central question is how to reuse sparse labels conservatively. We instantiate this setting with ACTive VLM-TTA for Online Retrieval (ACTOR), a training-free plug-in module that routes uncertain samples among base prediction, label query, and memory-based correction using a consistency-and-reliability check over previously queried labels. Across ten cross-domain benchmarks and a domain-transfer benchmark based on ImageNet, ACTOR consistently improves strong VLM-TTA baselines on ResNet and ViT backbones with negligible inference overhead. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/ACTOR.

URLs: https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/ACTOR.

cross The Invisible Gap: Romanized Indic Generation Fails Where Automatic Metrics Succeed

cross When Routine Operations Break Architectural Pattern Relationships in LLM Agent Harnesses

Authors: Boming Xia, Qinghua Lu, Dino Sejdinovic, Liming Zhu

Abstract: LLM agent harnesses distribute tool mediation, state management and safety guardrails across native services, independently written plugins and configuration. Routine operations can change the runtime order or retained state that architectural pattern relationships require, without any code change. We study five relationships associated with the Guardrail Enforcement Point, Autonomy Controller and Blackboard patterns across five released harnesses, analysing a main sample of fourteen independently written plugins. Using PatternOps, our assessment procedure, we trace requirements from pattern descriptions, provider documentation and deployment profiles to the providers that realise them and the records that decide them. We then compare alternative allocations under supported operations. Such operations broke relationships silently: after a plugin was reapplied or an allow rule saved, policy stopped evaluating edits while every plugin stayed active, and a prohibited edit executed. Which alternatives qualified depended on the requirement. Allocations that kept the required order, or kept or rebuilt the needed state, held on their tested paths and operation sequences, while others prevented prohibited effects but missed edits rejected elsewhere. cross AdaAlloc: Adaptive Visual Token Allocation for Long-Video Question Answering

Authors: Joungbin An, Kristen Grauman

Abstract: Long-video question answering forces multimodal large language models to reason under strict visual-token budgets, creating a tradeoff between broad temporal coverage and fine-grained spatial detail. Uniform sampling preserves coverage but often obscures decisive local cues, while caption-based or frame-similarity retrieval operates over lossy proxies that can miss motion, state changes, and before-after relations. We recast long-video reasoning as a visual-token allocation problem: given a fixed budget, decide how much to spend on global context versus high-resolution local evidence, and where in the video to draw that local evidence from. We introduce AdaAlloc, a training-free inference framework that adaptively allocates visual tokens between a global video overview and localized visual detail. AdaAlloc first plans a global/local allocation policy, then uses temporal grounding to locate candidate evidence segments and refines them into a compact temporal memory. The selected policy controls both frame sampling density and resolution, combining sparse, lower-resolution global frames with denser, higher-resolution local frames drawn from the refined memory. The final answerer reasons over the allocated global context and refined local evidence. Across LVBench, MLVU, VideoMME, and LongVideoBench, AdaAlloc improves over budget-matched uniform sampling by up to 10.5 percentage points and outperforms the compared frame-selection and agentic methods on their reported benchmarks.

cross AlienBench: A Benchmark of Structural Novelty in Language Model Outputs

Authors: Jonas Oppenlaender, Johanna M. Silvennoinen

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for creative tasks such as writing, design, and idea generation. For these uses, it matters whether a model's ideas depart from familiar examples or stay close to them. Novelty is one standard measure of creativity, and current creativity evaluations ask human experts or LLM judges to rate how novel an output is. However, these ratings have no fixed reference for what counts as novel (or, in other terms, departure from the familiar). Measuring this departure requires a task with known familiar defaults. The structured imagination task from cognitive psychology is such a task. In this creativity test, people imagine a creature from another planet. The departure from features of Earth animals, such as bilateral symmetry and legs as mode of locomotion, is used as measure of creativity. We introduce AlienBench, a benchmark that adapts the structured imagination task to language models. Models describe alien creatures, and a panel of three LLM judges extracts ten biological features from the description and labels them as either Earth-typical or a departure. The sum of feature departures is the score of a description, from 0 to 10. Each model describes alien creatures under six prompt conditions that vary the framing of the task and the planetary environment. Each model generates 30 descriptions per condition (180 in total), and its overall score is the mean across these descriptions. We evaluate 10 language models, from frontier to open-weight models. Overall scores range from 3.28 (GPT-6 Luna) to 6.55 (Ministral 3 14B). Interestingly, open-weight models score higher than proprietary models in this task (5.40 vs. 4.58). Under the baseline prompt, the models depart from Earth defaults more often on average than 100 human participants on every feature. We release all prompts, generations, judge outputs, and scores.

Abstract: Diffusion models have recently emerged as a powerful paradigm for generating 3D molecules. Abstract: LiDAR-based 3D Object Detection (L3OD) is a fundamental 3D perception task, yet its performance is often compromised by domain shifts arising from diverse sensor configurations and environmental conditions. cross SIFR: Stable Signed Flow Reinforcement

Authors: Runlong Liao, Xingqian Xu, Lizhang Chen, Baiyu Su, Yufan Huang, Zhonghao Wang, Akash Gokul, Ming-Yu Liu, Qiang Liu, Yogesh Balaji

Abstract: Reward-based fine-tuning of flow models requires effectively promoting high-reward and suppressing low-reward samples, while maintaining training stability over many rounds of iterative updates. Iterative reward fine-tuning provides a simple starting point, but the positive reward weights give no explicit unfitting signal, and repeated reweighting concentrates the distribution and causes collapse. We propose Stable Signed Flow Reinforcement (SIFR), which replaces the reward weights by signed advantages to supply the missing unfitting signal, and constrains the velocity drift from a reference model to stabilize the updates, with a Lagrange multiplier that adapts the regularization strength to a prescribed drift budget. We further show that existing flow RL methods fall within this framework: they implicitly minimize the signed loss, differing in their regularization choices. We validate SIFR on SD3.5 Medium (2.5B) and FLUX.2 Klein (9B) and scale it to Cosmos 3 Super Text2Image (64B): across all 12 backbone--reward pairs with preference and rule-based rewards, it matches or exceeds existing methods, reaches their peak rewards much faster, and keeps improving over long training where they plateau or collapse.

cross RPEG: Training Reasoning Language Models Scalably and Cost-Effectively

Authors: Maciej Besta, Leonard Schmidt, Pierre Pang, Patrik Okanovic, Robert Gerstenberger, Torsten Hoefler

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is now central to training Reasoning Language Models (RLMs), but RLM pipelines are difficult to scale and costly to run because each RL step couples multiple large models, heterogeneous stages, cross-step state, and complex asynchronous dependencies. Existing systems typically optimize only parts of this design space. We address this complexity by jointly optimizing the pipeline structure as well as its execution using a novel data-centric graph intermediate representation called RLM Pipeline Execution Graph (RPEG). RPEG exposes structural choices such as bounded asynchrony and actor-critic sharing together with execution optimizations such as stage fusion, fine-grained scheduling, and multi-dimensional model parallelism. It is coupled with a comprehensive analytical performance model for RLM training, jointly capturing compute, communication, memory, and hierarchical rack-, node-, and GPU-level effects. Across systems with up to 256 GH200 GPUs, our distributed runtime engine implementing RPEG improves performance by up to 2x over ReaL and reduces step latency by up to 2.48x over verl, while increasing hardware utilization by up to 30%. Overall, RPEG provides a graph-driven foundation for next-generation training pipelines for frontier reasoning LLMs.

cross On the Limits of Execution Traces for Repairing Assertion Failures in LLM-Generated Code

Authors: Muntasir Adnan, Carlos C. N. Kuhn

Abstract: Iterative repair returns a failed program's error to a large language model (LLM) and asks it to fix it. After iterative repair, the remaining failures are mostly assertion failures: with four Qwen2.5 models, they make up 79% of residual failures on BigCodeBench, all on HumanEval and 93% on LiveCodeBench. We argue, through an information view of the feedback, that an assertion failure says little about where the fault lies. We propose Breakpoint Debugging (BPD), which returns the failed program annotated inline with its variables' runtime values. We take the tasks that iterative repair failed to fix with five repair attempts and compare two ways of spending five further generations on them: BPD, and resampling. On BigCodeBench, BPD repairs 26.7% of these tasks against 22.4%, and its advantage grows with model size. On HumanEval, BPD outperforms resampling but only slightly, and on LiveCodeBench, fresh sampling repairs more (6.5% against 4.9%). Furthermore, BPD reduces unchanged-code resubmissions from 28% to 8% on BigCodeBench. Using only the first failing test's trace, it outperforms tracing all failing tests while using fewer tokens than both all-trace BPD and plain iterative repair. On LiveCodeBench, BPD gives no gain even on the public tests, so a visible failing input is not enough for a trace to help. These results match the information view: a trace helps only when the test exposes a failing input, and the fault is an implementation slip; otherwise, a repair loop should resample rather than trace.

cross BOXBOX: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Long-Running Performance Optimization

Authors: Yuhao Tan, Hanyun Jiang, Fangkai Yang, Yuan Yao, Taolue Chen, Bo Qiao, Yu Kang, Lu Wang, Pu Zhao, Xiaoxing Ma, Qingwei Lin

Abstract: Automated harness optimization improves agents by learning from trajectories and task feedback. Existing offline methods use reference answers to optimize harnesses for reuse on held-out tasks. However, many real-world tasks are open-ended, while long runs expose new difficulties that offline-optimized harnesses may not address. To study harness optimization in these settings, we introduce PerfRace, a benchmark for long-running performance optimization of software projects, and investigate whether agents can use task-specific experience to recursively improve their harnesses during an ongoing task. To this end, we introduce BOXBOX, which enables agent-triggered harness revision and kernel-managed hot reloading while preserving execution context. cross HMMF-Net: A Lightweight Hierarchical Multi-Frame Multimodal Fusion Network for Accurate Fish Feeding Intensity Assessment

Authors: Phi-Hung Hoang, Van-Manh Tran, Nam-Thuan Trinh, Thi-Thu-Hong Phan

Abstract: Automated fish feeding intensity assessment (FFIA) is important for precision aquaculture and feed cost optimization. However, unimodal methods are vulnerable to acoustic noise and visual disturbances, while existing multimodal frameworks often rely on computationally intensive 3D convolutions or Transformers, limiting their use on edge devices. To address these limitations, we propose HMMF-Net, a lightweight multimodal architecture that combines early multi-frame visual encoding and multi-axis acoustic modeling through a hierarchical bidirectional messenger cross-modal fusion (BMCF) mechanism. The visual branch captures temporal variations in feeding behavior by stacking temporally distant frames as a multi-channel input to an adapted 2D EfficientNetB0 backbone. The acoustic branch employs adaptive multi-axis residual units (AMARU) to extract temporal, frequency, and local time-frequency representations from log-mel spectrograms. BMCF progressively exchanges information between the two modalities at intermediate representation levels through compact messenger tokens, reducing the computational overhead of dense cross-modal attention. Evaluated on the AV-FFIA dataset using five-fold cross-validation, HMMF-Net achieves 96.77% accuracy and 96.88% F1-score on the held-out test folds, with 3.987M parameters and 1.002 GFLOPs. Compared with the best-performing visual configuration (95.49% accuracy), HMMF-Net reduces the classification error by 28.38%. Deployment on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano using TensorRT with FP32 precision maintains comparable accuracy, with an inference time of 11.54 ms per 2-second sample and a model size of 18.81 MB. These results demonstrate that HMMF-Net combines effective multimodal representation learning with computational efficiency, enabling accurate and practical FFIA on resource-constrained edge platforms.

cross FondueQA: A Benchmark for Evaluating Parametric Compensation in Multi-Hop QA

Authors: Laurin Schmid, Suman Navaratnarajah, Koel Dutta Chowdhury, Steffen Eger, Josif Grabocka, Christian Frey

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) show remarkable performance based on parametric knowledge, i.e., their ability to recall facts and associations internalized in a model's weights. However, studying this capability on established multi-hop benchmarks may be complicated by benchmark contamination, as many of these benchmarks have been publicly available long enough to plausibly appear in modern pretraining corpora. As a result, correct answers under missing evidence may reflect either general parametric knowledge or memorization of benchmark-specific instances. To study how models use internal knowledge when retrieval evidence is incomplete, we introduce FondueQA, in which every required multi-hop fact is uniquely localized to a designated evidence document. This enables controlled document-level evidence ablations to study parametric compensation under incomplete retrieval. Our empirical evaluation reveals that parametric compensation strongly depends on both entity popularity and the structural role of missing evidence. Generally, models compensate more successfully for facts involving popular entities. On FondueQA, we show that bridging evidence can be more useful than the answer-bearing document itself. Moreover, models may recall a missing fact in isolation but fail to use it under partial evidence, particularly when the context contains plausible competing entities, which can reduce Exact Match by up to 15 percentage points.

cross AudioSphere: Towards Self-Supervised Spatial Audio Representation Models

Authors: Goksenin Yuksel, Marcel van Gerven, Kiki van der Heijden

Abstract: Recent audio embedding models learn representations that transfer across clip- and frame-level tasks, yet they operate almost exclusively on single-channel signals and are blind to spatial structure. Spatial audio models capture that structure but rely on clip-level supervision or contrastive objectives, yielding embeddings unsuitable for frame-level tasks like sound event detection and localization (SELD). We argue this dichotomy is not fundamental: patch-level spatial self-supervision can jointly learn acoustic and spatial features at both temporal granularities. We introduce AudioSphere, a multi-channel masked autoencoder trained on Ambisonics recordings that reconstructs both spectral content and directional cues via a joint spatiotemporal masking strategy that masks both time-frequency patches and intensity vectors. AudioSphere supports frame-level predictions while producing strong clip-level representations via aggregation. Alongside the model, we release RealSELD, a standardized benchmark for evaluating frozen backbones on real-world multi-channel SELD. AudioSphere enables, for the first time, SELD with a frozen self-supervised backbone, while matching or exceeding prior self-supervised audio embedding models on clip-level classification tasks spanning environmental sound, speech, and music, and on frame-level sound event detection. All code and data are available at https://github.com/labhamlet/AudioSphere and https://huggingface.co/labhamlet.

URLs: https://github.com/labhamlet/AudioSphere, https://huggingface.co/labhamlet.

cross Diversity-Aware Client Selection for Communication-Efficient Federated Learning

Authors: Max Halleryd, Sima Sinaei, Viktor Valadi, Mina Alibeigi, Henrik Abrahamsson, Salman Toor

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across distributed clients without sharing raw data, making it attractive for privacy-sensitive and bandwidth-constrained applications. A practical challenge in deploying FL is reducing communication overhead while maintaining model performance under heterogeneous, non-IID data distributions. Selecting which subset of clients participates in each training round offers a principled way to address both challenges simultaneously, yet its impact on data diversity and representativeness remains underexplored. In this paper, we investigate three client selection strategies, Power-of-Choice, Fisher information, and Centered Kernel Alignment, evaluating them not only by accuracy and convergence, but through the lens of participation diversity using the Gini coefficient and KL divergence. Under non-IID conditions, restricting participation to 15% of clients per round achieves significant communication savings, and when selection promotes diverse and balanced contributions, it also improves generalization over biased alternatives. This work provides a systematic and reproducible analysis demonstrating that diversity-focused client selection renders communication efficiency and model quality complementary rather than competing objectives.

cross A Stochastic Latent Variable Generalization of Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures

Authors: Nikos Giakoumoglou, Paschalis Giakoumoglou, Tania Stathaki

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) predict the latent embedding of a target signal from a context signal, with an auxiliary variable z conditioning the predictor. Despite its prominent role in the original JEPA blueprint, the content of z has been carried by a fixed deterministic token in every prior instantiation, collapsing the genuinely multi-modal distribution over plausible completions to a point estimate. We present S-JEPA, which realises z as a proper stochastic latent variable for masked image prediction: a learned posterior infers z from context and target during training, while a learned context-conditioned prior approximates the distribution of plausible completions at inference, with no decoders and no reconstruction. In its deterministic limit the objective reduces to the I-JEPA loss in functional form, generalising that objective rather than strictly containing the I-JEPA model. Our central finding is that stochasticity adds capabilities, error-correlated predictive uncertainty and diverse completions, at no cost to representation quality: under matched architectures and schedules S-JEPA improves over the deterministic I-JEPA baseline and exceeds LeJEPA at matched architecture, while additionally producing spatially structured uncertainty maps and semantically diverse completions, neither of which the deterministic predictor produces.

cross Robust Deep Prototype Classifiers via Lipschitz Training

Authors: Adia Khalid Khan, Lukas Gehring, Benjamin Paa{\ss}en

Abstract: Robustness and interpretability are interrelated and desirable properties of machine learning models. A model that changes its prediction under imperceptible perturbations behaves inconsistently with human-recognizable features and, hence, is not interpretable. Prototype-based classifiers such as Generalized Learning Vector Quantization address both at once. They are interpretable by construction because they make the decision by comparing the input to the prototypical example; and their hypothesis margin yields a closed form robustness certificate. However, on high-dimensional data, these shallow models need deep embedding networks to achieve high accuracy, and this voids the robustness certificate. The margin is a statement about distances in the embedding space, and an unconstrained network can map an arbitrarily small input perturbation to arbitrarily large attacks in the embedding space. We close this gap with a theorem: if the embedding network is locally L-Lipschitz around a point, and the classifier is {\epsilon}-robust around its embedding, then the full model is {\epsilon}/L-robust in the input space. Based on this, we propose a Lipschitz training scheme to achieve L-Lipschitz properties for embedding networks. We evaluate three classification heads (linear, linear Support Vector Machine, and GLVQ) crossed with two embedding networks (standard and Lipschitz-constrained) on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Imagenette-160, under FGS, PGD and C\&W attacks. The evaluation results show that combining a prototype-based head with a Lipschitz-constrained embedding provides the strongest robustness. This suggests that the Lipschitz constraint provides an additional robustness benefit in addition to the advantage provided by the prototype-based head. Since the Lipschitz constraint is enforced by sampling rather than by construction, the reported robustness is empirical rather than certified.

cross Syn2DRE: Synergistic Dual Teacher-Student Framework for Document-level Relation Extraction

Abstract: Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) aims to identify relations between entities across a document but typically relies on costly large-scale annotations. While pseudo-labelling enables the use of low-quality data, existing methods often suffer from inefficiency and confirmation bias. To address these challenges, we propose Syn2DRE, a semi-supervised framework that combines self-supervised learning with cross-supervision in a lightweight teacher-student architecture to produce more reliable pseudo-labels. Additionally, we introduce a unified end-to-end training strategy to further improve efficiency of the framework. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that Syn2DRE consistently outperforms prior state-of-the-art semi-supervised DocRE methods.

cross When Feedback Becomes a Backdoor: Tracing Backdoor Skill Formation in Text-Space Skill Optimization

Authors: Louise Nayeong Koh, Yaechan Lee, Myeongjong Lee, Doyeon Kim, Juyoung Park, Seunggyu Kim, Inseo Kim, Chaerin Jung, Hyogon Ryu

Abstract: Reference answers are a supervision channel in text-space Skill optimization, which produces reusable text artifacts called Skills that guide future agent behavior. Poisoning those answers can induce a persistent trigger-conditioned rule without giving the attacker access to the model, optimizer, or Skill file. We study this reference-feedback poisoning in SkillOpt by tracing an attacker-supplied trigger--payload mapping from reflection to held-out activation. In SearchQA, where the payload replaces a short answer, the natural attack retains an exact rule in all three seeds; each final Skill emits the payload on 200/200 triggered questions and 0/200 clean questions. In seed 42, removing the rule eliminates activation, and an in-run comparison shows similar clean accuracy to benign SkillOpt (169/200 versus 171/200). In SpreadsheetBench, where the payload requires an additional workbook edit, no natural run retains an exact rule across three seeds. Diagnostic interventions locate two successive bottlenecks: the Analyst abstracts away required literals, and, once literal preservation is enabled, edit-budget ranking can remove exact edits before candidate construction. Poisoned references can therefore create persistent backdoors, but exposure alone does not ensure that the induced rule is formed, selected, and retained.

cross HiMEM: Hippocampus-Inspired Memory Consolidation, Pruning, and Composition for Vision-Language-Action Models in 3D Scenes

Authors: Boran Zhao, Yanbin Hu, Jun Ye, Zhenxian Hu, Yuxin Zhai, Jin Cui, Pengju Ren

Abstract: Memory plays a crucial role in long-horizon task execution and cross-task generalization for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models operating in complex 3D scenes.
