We illustrate the sandbox's utility in a first suite of experiments on injecting new knowledge through post-training and in-context learning. These methods let LittleLearner better utilize existing knowledge, but do not raise out-of-scope capabilities. Our findings underscore the value of this controlled environment for future investigations.

replace-cross Discretizing Continuous Time Series for Imputation with Masked Diffusion Training

Authors: Dongbin Kim, Seungyun Lee, Geonwoo Shin, Jaewook Lee

Abstract: Time series imputation is a crucial area for reliable time series analysis, yet it remains challenging due to the complex temporal dynamics and noise of real-world data. Existing approaches, however, exhibit two limitations: missing and observed values are embedded within the same representation space without explicit structural separation, and continuous diffusion-based methods recover the missing values only through many iterative denoising steps rather than by predicting them directly. To address these, we propose the Masked Diffusion Time-series Imputation Model (MDTIM), which leverages the training paradigm of masked diffusion model for imputation tasks. The [MASK] token is structurally orthogonal to valid observations, and because the corrupted state is the partially observed series itself, the model is trained to predict the missing values directly from the observed ones, naturally aligning both the representation and the learning objective with the imputation task. To bridge the gap between discrete masked diffusion and the continuous, ordinal nature of time series, we further introduce Stochastic Discretization, which maps continuous values to ordinal-aware tokens while preserving continuous dynamics. Our experiments on diverse benchmarks confirm that MDTIM achieves superior robustness and scalability, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines across various missing scenarios. Code and project page are available at https://dongbeank.github.io/MDTIM/.

URLs: https://dongbeank.github.io/MDTIM/.

replace-cross Finding and using interpretable latents in a neutrino foundation model with sparse autoencoders

Authors: Rapha\"el Bonnet-Guerrini, Johann Ioannou-Nikolaides, Inar Timiryasov, Vincenzo Piuri

Abstract: We present a first application of sparse-autoencoder-based mechanistic interpretability to particle physics. Studying a neutrino foundation model pretrained on IceCube data and fine-tuned for direction reconstruction, we identify a validated atlas of physical concepts in the model representation, using a strict validation protocol consisting of held-out tests, matched nuisance controls, and replication across independent dictionary trainings. Causal interventions show that the direction head barely draws on this atlas. Motivated by this underused information, we train an uncertainty head on the same event-level representation to predict the model's angular reconstruction error. Unlike the direction head, it depends causally on quality and brightness features from the atlas. These results suggest that mechanistic interpretability can reveal learned latent physics encoded within a model's internal representation and help design downstream tasks that exploit it.

replace-cross Benchmark Contamination: A Taxonomy Organized by Defeated Mitigation

Authors: Johanna Angulo, V\'ictor Yeste, Hector Espinos-Morato

Abstract: A benchmark score is a joint property of the model, the evaluation harness, the elicitation budget, the sampled population, and contamination status. Leaderboards publish the model and the score, so capability and leakage stay observationally equivalent. Existing taxonomies classify contamination for automated detection, not the question a reporter faces at publication: given the mitigations already applied, which validity threats remain open? We introduce a taxonomy organized by the mitigation each type defeats -- direct, derivative, temporal, distributional, and acquired -- spanning training-time and evaluation-time leakage. Holding out a private test set closes the first alone. The fifth is acquired during the evaluation itself; because it is a property of one run, it must be recorded with the reported score rather than with the benchmark release. We operationalize it as a four-field disclosure protocol in which "unknown" is a valid entry, released under CC BY 4.0 with a JSON Schema, a validator, and worked examples. Two coders external to the design team applied a pre-registered instrument to 41 documents. Per-variable linear-weighted $\kappa$ runs from 0.00 to 0.35 (median 0.21) over 29 main-pass documents against a single-coder test-retest ceiling of 0.84, collapsing under the class skew the registration anticipated; pooling raises it to 0.46 through chance correction rather than better agreement. Two variables fall below the prevalence-robust threshold registered in advance: strata reporting and the acquired type introduced here. Disagreement concentrates on when a variable applies rather than on what a document states. Elicitation budgets are reported in 13% of documents, and no document addresses all five types. The contribution is the taxonomy, the score-side artifact that follows from it, and a pre-registered measurement of instrument reliability and current disclosure.

replace-cross BiG-SURE - Bipartite Graph for Semantic Uncertainty and Reliability Estimation of LLMs

Authors: Debarpan Bhattacharya, Malay Phadke, Sriram Ganapathy

Abstract: Reliable uncertainty estimation is a crucial requirement for deploying large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) in safety-critical settings, especially when the model parameters are not accessible (black-box). We propose BiG-SURE, an uncertainty estimator based on cross-temperature semantic agreement. The method samples low-temperature responses as stable semantic anchors and high-temperature responses as probes under meaning-preserving input transformations. It then constructs an anchor-probe Bipartite Graph (BiG) using NLI-based entailment scores and defines confidence through the normalized squared spectral energy of this matrix, with uncertainty given by its complement. This bipartite graph-based Semantic Uncertainty and Reliability Estimation (SURE) score measures whether high-temperature probes remain semantically aligned with the model's stable low-temperature belief or not. We evaluate BiG-SURE on text QA, multilingual QA, and multimodal QA tasks across multiple model families. In these experiments, BiG-SURE improves average abstention AUROC over prior black-box uncertainty estimators, while remaining simple, unsupervised, and applicable to black-box model settings.

replace-cross Anatomy of LLM Sycophancy: What a Flip Rate Hides

Authors: Haonan Huang, Tianlin Zhang

Abstract: A model under pushback can correct itself, capitulate, or hold, and one flip rate counts a correction and a capitulation alike. SycoLens, a modular replay protocol, measures flip rates from replays of an item, a committed answer, and one scripted user line, each against a matched control with the line deleted. Pushback wording, committed text, answer format, boundary distance, and ground truth become factors of one instrument, where earlier instruments vary one to three, so the interactions between the factors become measurable. Across eleven frontier models and about 760,000 controlled replays, lines that assert the opposite verdict and lines that challenge without asserting one rank the models almost unrelatedly. Flip effects grow several-fold near a model's boundary, yet items the model is certain of still carry about half of the most-affected totals; planted prose anchors a commitment at rest more than it protects it. Under a yes/no readout the rankings come closer, entangled with a pressure-induced shift toward "no". Where a truth exists, one model re-derives and corrects itself while another abandons correct answers without written work. One score per model therefore compares different behaviours; we condense them into a reporting profile, and the records and analyses will be released upon publication.

replace-cross Learning to Simulate Individuals from Macro Social Signals

Authors: Yining Zhao, Bushi Liu, Haofei Yu, Zhengyang Qi, Shanyong Wang, Chuyue Li, Yuxiang Liu, Jiaxuan You

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate how individuals respond to new situations, yet the behavioral reasoning behind these responses is either inherited from pretraining or learned from individual-level annotations, which offer limited behavioral diversity and little supervision of the reasoning itself. We propose to learn behavioral reasoning from prediction markets, whose price trajectories record how populations respond to real-world events at scale. We introduce macro2mind, which trains a language model with GRPO using market signals. A social behavioral decomposition makes behavioral reasoning an explicit step of forecasting: the model infers representative groups of market participants, predicts how each interprets the news and updates its beliefs, reasons about their interactions, and aggregates these responses into a price. A hindsight-regret curriculum with difficulty-aware sampling focuses training on transitions where hindsight-identified groups substantially improve the forecast while prioritizing examples that remain learnable for the current policy. The learned reasoning applies to user simulation without further training. On SWM-Bench, macro2mind achieves state-of-the-art directional accuracy and correlation on Polymarket. Trained on market data, it transfers zero-shot to four user-simulation benchmarks (Humanual, OvertonBench, PRISM, and CAD) and has competitive performance among zero-shot methods. Used as a data generator, macro2mind also raises a downstream simulator's accuracy on unseen users by 15.5 points, outperforming data generated by its backbone by 13.2 points.

replace-cross VOMMI: Collecting and Leveraging Portable Demonstrations for Mobile Manipulation

Authors: Yutian Zhang, Xingrui Xiong, Siyuan Ma, Donghao Wang, Yang Li, Jiawen Wen, Jiaqi Zhai, Liwen Yang, Ce Hao, Haozhen Chi, Yangkun Zhu, Yifan Zhu, Xiaowen Chu, Dong Wei, Qiaojun Yu, Dibo Hou

Abstract: Portable mobile-manipulation demonstrations can help alleviate data scarcity for embodied intelligence, but obtaining reliable, low-cost, and robot-free motion supervision from RGB observations remains challenging. Existing approaches often rely on teleoperation or specialized devices equipped with additional sensing hardware, while directly using estimated visual odometry (VO) trajectories can introduce inconsistencies due to accumulated drift and imperfect motion supervision. We present the Visual-Odometry-Conditioned Mobile Manipulation Interface (VOMMI), a portable demonstration collection and learning framework that connects portable RGB demonstrations to vision-language-action (VLA) post-training through offline trajectory reconstruction and online visual-motion conditioning. VOMMI synchronizes body and hand views to capture navigation context and local object interactions without requiring human-robot kinematic correspondence calibration. R2-VO refines offline demonstration trajectories using sparse geometric anchors and produces causal local-motion tokens over multiple prediction horizons for online policy conditioning. An action-group residual adapter incorporates these tokens only into the base branch. Experiments use a 500-trajectory portable for each task, with 75 trajectories held out for RGB-VO evaluation, and 200 robot demonstrations as references. Our policy, post-trained only on portable demonstrations, achieves 18.2% lower base-velocity error than a policy trained with robot-collected demonstrations, while maintaining comparable end-effector translation accuracy. Offline reconstruction reduces absolute trajectory errors for the body and hand streams by 24.6% on average relative to the best evaluated baseline for each stream. The complete system improves the mean success rate by 8.3 percentage points over OpenPI 0.5 across three real-robot tasks.

replace-cross MetaEncoder: Exploring the Limit of Bi-Encoders for Multimodal System One Decision Making with Natural Language Interface

Authors: Jianpeng Cheng, Guangyu Sun, Aashu Singh, Benyu Zhang, Haixing Dai, Hossein Mansour, Jiangfan Zhang, Shlok Kumar Mishra, Wei Sun, Xuanming Cui, Yanli Liu, Qi Guo, Max Xiangjun Fan, Jun Xiao

Abstract: System One models prioritize fast, low-latency decision-making and calibrated probability distributions over token generation. While prevailing systems rely on structured schemas and typed outputs, candidate-scoring trade-offs remain poorly understood. We systematically investigate the scaling dynamics, accuracy, and inference efficiency of three scoring architectures adapted from a pre-trained decoder: bi-encoders, cross-encoders, and constrained decoders. Experiments show clear performance scaling with model size across all three architectures. Cross-encoders consistently achieve the highest accuracy across model sizes, while bi-encoders and constrained decoders perform similarly as training tokens scale. Efficiency-wise, bi-encoders maintain near-constant inference latency as candidate pools grow because candidate embeddings can be cached offline, whereas cross-encoder and constrained-decoder costs scale quadratically with candidate size. This efficiency advantage makes bi-encoders uniquely suited to bridge small closed-set decisions and massive open-set retrieval. For closed-set tasks, including all candidate options in the request prompt is critical for bi-encoder competitiveness. Guided by these insights, we introduce MetaEncoder, a multimodal encoder fine-tuned from a Muse-Glimmer decoder via unidirectional contrastive learning to match requests and candidates. We present packed contrastive training to maximize efficiency for dynamically allocated negative candidates, enabling memory-efficient full fine-tuning of a 30B decoder into an encoder. Evaluated across 12 benchmark suites spanning closed-set decision-making, multimodal understanding, and open-set retrieval, MetaEncoder outperforms state-of-the-art multimodal embedding baselines on decision tasks and MMEB-v3 image/video retrieval, while scaling to open candidate spaces far too large for current decision models.

replace-cross Diffusion-Steering-Based MPC with a JEPA World Action Model

Authors: Shashank Hegde, Alexander Popov, Elie Aljalbout, Nikolai Smolyanskiy

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) predict actions and future observations, typically from a reconstruction-based representation that carries noisy, redundant information which can complicate downstream predictions. We introduce LeWAM, a bidirectional transformer for forward, backward, inverse dynamics and policy prediction, on a decoder-free JEPA latent trained end-to-end through all four modes. We see the following benefits: 1) Alignment: linear probes read robot and object state from LeWAM's latent better than from a regular Le World Model (a forward-only JEPA world model), while the latent ignores visual distractors as well as LeWM does and far better than a reconstruction-based WAM. 2) Acting: Closed-loop evaluations of LeWAM match a regular flow-matching policy trained on the same encoder at matched size, while also providing a world model. 3) Planning: Sampling raw actions when planning with WAMs lets MPC exploit dynamics-model inaccuracies; planning in the noise space of the policy head instead improves the closed-loop performance of these WAMs.

replace-cross Adversarial Detection for Pre-trained Classifiers via Lipschitz-Margin Analysis with Certification

Authors: Rui Zhang, Youwen Zhu, Xinle Cao, Huazhen Yu, Jian Liu

Abstract: Deep classifiers deployed in safety-critical services like autonomous-driving perception remain vulnerable to small adversarial perturbations. Certified $\ell_2$ defenses today face a costly trade-off: randomized smoothing (RS) requires hundreds of noisy queries per input, while Lipschitz networks (LN) constrain the architecture and sacrifice clean accuracy. We take a detection-oriented approach instead: rather than certifying that predictions are invariant inside an $\ell_2$ ball, we certify that adversarial inputs within an $\ell_2$ ball are \emph{detected} --- a guarantee aligned with security-critical deployments where missing an attack is costlier than flagging a clean input. To realize this, we prove a Lipschitz-margin reduction from certified $\ell_2$ robustness to certified detection, which reduces the problem to handling a single Lipschitz constant $L$. We introduce two detector families: $\mathbb{D}_{Adv}$, which estimates $L$ from a single defender-side perturbed pair, and $\mathbb{D}_{lc}$, which augments that pair with a local margin centroid to keep the estimate above what the certificate requires. Both deploy on any pretrained classifier without retraining. With a valid global bound, the detector is fully certified at that radius; with a local estimate $L'$, certification is sample-wise and applies only when our certified condition holds. Across MNIST, CIFAR-10, and ImageNet-1k on four backbones (LeNet-5, VGG16, ResNet-50, ViT-B/16), our detectors match or outperform five baselines (CTT, SortNet, CRM, ContraNet, A$^2$D). For CIFAR-10 with 1-Lipschitz SortNet, our reduction delivers $100\%$ certified detection at the lowest FPR among compared certified detectors; on the standard CIFAR-10 VGG16, our detectors reject all evaluated in-budget adversarial examples, with 62-82\% both rejected and formally certified.

replace-cross PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Han Shen, Qifu Tyler Sun, Fanyang Meng, Ye Wang, Yongsheng Liang

Abstract: Collaborative edge large language model (LLM) systems often require repeated prefill computation when task contexts are transferred between agents. Reusing previously computed key--value (KV) caches can eliminate this overhead, but requires KV-cache delivery before the receiving agent begins decoding. Under strict deadlines and unreliable edge links, complete KV cache transfer may be infeasible, making the timely delivery of useful KV information critical. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that combines prioritized source transmission with adaptive loss recovery. PriorityFEC first organizes KV blocks into a utility-aware source queue to prioritize important information. It then employs ACK-sliding Streaming FEC and a lightweight Double Deep Q-Network (Double-DQN) scheduler to dynamically balance source delivery and repair transmission under deadline constraints. We implement PriorityFEC in a distributed edge LLM-serving prototype and evaluate it using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple workloads. Experimental results demonstrate that PriorityFEC preserves more useful KV information and achieves substantially higher task accuracy than competing transport schemes, particularly when the deadline is insufficient to deliver the complete KV cache.

replace-cross Explore Separately, Adopt Selectively: Execution-Guided Tool Use over Large Tool Libraries

Authors: Anna Mazhar, Seiji Maekawa, Nikita Bhutani

Abstract: Enterprise LLM agents using growing tool catalogs must surface capabilities as workflows evolve without flooding context or committing to the wrong tool. Adaptive retrieval can expose new capabilities, but when several tools seem plausible, probing them sequentially can stop at an early failure or leave failed attempts in the agent context. We present BRIA (BRanch-Inspect-Adopt), a plug-and-play method that investigates candidates in isolated branches. Each branch retrieves and executes tools independently. A task agent inspects the execution evidence and selectively adopts useful results in its context. BRIA also retrieves tools as new capability needs arise. To evaluate BRIA in an enterprise setting, we assemble a single catalog with 1897 tools from PlanBench-XL and MCP-Atlas. We compare BRIA with static and adaptive baselines using three LLM families. We observe that BRIA lies on the accuracy-cost frontier. Compared with the strongest baseline, BRIA uses less than half as many input tokens to achieve equal or higher success rates on PlanBench-XL, and improves task performance by up to 14.4 percentage points on MCP-Atlas. For enterprise agents over large catalogs, these results suggest that candidate tools can be effectively explored with execution evidence without carrying every result in the agent's context.

replace-cross The As-If Machine: Personalized AI counterfactuals make distant policy risks feel close

Authors: Joshua Ashkinaze, Yubo Zhou, Pengyu Chen, Avi Bhattacharya, Hyebin Park, Yanman Zhang, Stephanie Preston, Eric Gilbert, Ceren Budak

Abstract: Life experiences can shape policy attitudes, but we only live one life. Citizens may therefore fail to support policies addressing risks they have never personally experienced. Narratives can make such risks immediate and personal, but personalized stories remain difficult to produce at scale. Here we ask whether AI can make distant scenarios feel concrete by simulating a participant's life as if that scenario occurred. We present the As-If Machine (AIM), a multi-agent system through which people co-write personalized counterfactual narratives---stories about their own lives under different policy futures. In two pre-registered and quota-matched experiments on science funding (N=1,007) and satellite cybersecurity (N=894), we compared AIM with two controls: reading an expert-written vignette on the same topic and co-writing with AIM about an unrelated topic. AIM increased psychological closeness---how close a risk feels to the self---by nearly identical margins in the two domains (0.36 and 0.38 standard deviations (SDs) against the vignette, 0.80 and 0.90 against unrelated writing). AIM increased psychological closeness even among participants with unfavorable views of AI. For policy attitudes, AIM's effects were 0.16 SDs against the vignette and 0.10 SDs (n.s.) against unrelated writing for science funding; the corresponding effects for satellite cybersecurity were 0.54 and 0.67 SDs. Across 23,496 human-AI autocomplete interactions, participants and the system played distinct roles: AIM supplied concrete detail that grounded the scenario, and participants supplied first-person perspective and emotion. Co-writing with the As-If Machine makes distant policy risks feel close.

replace-cross What Makes a Question Curious? Measuring Model Curiosity in Human-LLM Interactions

Authors: Angana Borah, Rachana Ramchandar, Inderjeet Nair, Rada Mihalcea

Abstract: Large language models increasingly act as partners in everyday communication. In these interactions, useful assistance may require not only answering, but also asking the right questions that surface knowledge gaps, assumptions, and alternatives. In this study, we refer to such epistemic question-asking as model curiosity, and introduce a theory-grounded taxonomy that distinguishes curiosity-oriented questions from generic model questioning. Across four conversation datasets, we find that curiosity-oriented questions remain rare (17-28% of all questions), but are associated with more exploratory and epistemically rich subsequent user responses, with varying patterns across datasets. We further conduct a human study to evaluate the usefulness of model curiosity, finding that curiosity can encourage exploration, especially in advice, creative, and research contexts. These findings have implications for building LLMs that ask more selectively to support deeper exploration and human-AI collaboration.

replace-cross Making Sense of Audience Change: Causal Attribution and Boundary Management on Xiaohongshu

Authors: Erin Zhou, Kyrie Zhou

Abstract: Recommendation-driven platforms can reshape not only what users see, but also who becomes visible and how community boundaries are negotiated. We examine Xiaohongshu discussions surrounding a perceived World Cup-related audience influx to understand how users attributed community change and how those explanations related to boundary-management strategies. We analyzed 262 comments from eight high-engagement threads using researcher-led thematic analysis, structured coding, and permutation-based association analysis. Users described personalization as both social separation and a boundary that could fail, interpreted platform growth through audience acquisition and commercialization, and contested whether gender should define community membership. Among 70 comments with explicit attributions and strategies, attribution and strategy were strongly associated (Cramer's V = .583): algorithmic attributions concentrated in technical boundary management, while social-actor attributions more often aligned with social enforcement or normative socialization. replace-cross Prompt Lottery in LLM-as-a-Judge: Measuring and Mitigating Sensitivity to Syntactic Rephrasing

Authors: Ilija Subasic, Brett Levert, Andrew Rabinovich, Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Zhao Chen

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge has become a standard for evaluating large language models, yet most approaches implicitly assume that a single judge prompt functions as a stable evaluation instrument. Prior work has primarily explored deliberate prompt modifications such as scoring rubrics, role descriptions, or chain-of-thought reasoning in order to optimize accuracy. In contrast, we investigate whether validated prompt variants intended to preserve evaluation semantics while differing in surface realization (\textit{paraphrastic rephrasings}) yield consistent judgments under a fixed evaluation task. We introduce \textit{Prompt Lottery}, a phenomenon in which paraphrastic rephrasings produce different LLMaJ judgments. Across a large-scale study spanning diverse evaluation tasks, judge families, and model backbones, we find that these variations can alter evaluation outcomes and, in some settings, reverse relative model rankings. A user study with 10 human annotators shows that LLMaJ judges exhibit 6-9$\times$ larger agreement degradation under prompt rephrasings than humans, falling below the generally accepted threshold in usability studies. This indicates that single-prompt evaluation is insufficiently robust for model ranking. Finally, we show that multi-prompt aggregation substantially improves robustness by emphasizing judgments that remain consistent across prompt variants.

replace-cross LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction

Authors: Xiaomeng Xiong, Marcus Walker, Yanlin Jiang

Abstract: Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. Prior work has largely relied on linguistic features or text representations derived from general-purpose and domain-specific language models, but predictive performance has remained modest. This study takes a different approach by using difficulty-related signals that emerge when open-weight large language models (LLMs) interact with test items. Using USMLE and PANCE medical licensure examination items, we examined LLMs in two roles. As judges, the models provided absolute difficulty ratings and pairwise difficulty comparisons. As simulated examinees, they generated response-based signals including correctness, keyed-option probability, response entropy, and the probability margin between the top two options. We then combined the strongest judgment and response signals with baseline item features in downstream prediction models. Pairwise difficulty judgment was the most consistent individual LLM signal across both datasets and outperformed absolute ratings, and adding simulated-examinee signals yielded only small further gains. The best configurations reached a Spearman correlation of $\rho$ = .447 on USMLE, exceeding the best system in the 2024 BEA shared task ($\rho$ = .25), and $\rho$ = .494 on PANCE. These results were comparable to direct absolute judgments from the proprietary frontier model Claude Opus 5 ($\rho$ = .443 and .488, respectively). Overall, judgment and response signals from open-weight LLMs captured difficulty-related information beyond text representations. Because these models can be deployed locally, the approach offers a practical option for predicting the difficulty of unreleased or high-security test items.

replace-cross STAR: Steering Action Representations with Proprioception for VLA Inference

Authors: Guangyu Zhao, Yongchang Zhang, Haoxuan Ru, Kaichen He, Haoran Wen, Kun Zhan, Zeyu Zhang, Yin Li, Yiwu Zhong

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) map language instructions and visual observations to robot actions, showing strong capabilities across robotic manipulation tasks. However, in our probing experiment, we find that action representations near the action output do not fully preserve task-relevant visual grounding. Motivated by this late-layer grounding gap, we introduce STAR, for STeering Action Representations, a training-free inference method for pre-trained VLAs. STAR applies an exponential moving average over late-layer residual updates, allowing later action representations to inherit object-aware update directions from earlier layers without model updates or architectural changes. It further uses recent proprioceptive states to adapt the mixing strength, using conservative mixing when execution dynamics change rapidly. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show that STAR improves late-layer object awareness and increases average task success rates by 4.0 percentage points. Our code is available at https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/STAR.

URLs: https://github.com/PKU-VaLuE-Lab/STAR.

replace-cross When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents

Authors: Xinyu Wang, Andres Fielbaum

Abstract: The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. Rebalancing policies that perform equally well under full compliance can thus show opposite results under mandatory disclosure. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.

replace-cross Behavior Graphs: Unsupervised Graph Structures for Interpreting Robot Deployment Data

Authors: Erik Bauer, Jakob Thumm, Christopher Agia, Rohan Sinha, Chen Xu, Haruki Nishimura, Jeannette Bohg, Marco Pavone, Masha Itkina

Abstract: Data flywheels promise continuous improvement via insights from deployment data. Understanding how the deployed model succeeds and fails can steer subsequent data collection towards patching failures and reinforcing successes. However, robot deployments create large volumes of high-dimensional rollout data, which is challenging to understand and confidently ground data collection decisions in. To make deployment data easier to analyze and act on, we propose behavior graphs, which compress policy rollouts into a single interpretable, directed graph. We construct the graphs by learning to identify similar behavior modes across rollouts, connecting the behaviors temporally, and finally linking them to outcomes. In a user study on three real-world tasks, where participants analyzed rollouts to choose a data collection strategy, 16 of 18 preferred behavior graphs to rollout videos, reaching comparable conclusions with 40.3% lower perceived temporal demand and 37.6% lower effort. Beyond interpretability for deployment data, behavior graphs are capable progress predictors: across 43 datasets, they improve the rank correlation between predicted progress and true temporal order by 22.9% and reduce mean absolute error by 53.5% relative to the strongest baseline. As the number of deployed robots grows, behavior graphs lend structure to high-dimensional policy rollouts and make deployment data easier to interpret and reason about.

replace-cross A Spectral Renormalization-Group View of Learning

Authors: Charles H. Martin

Abstract: We study how the layers in deep neural networks (NNs) converge, using Wilsonian Renormalization-Group (RG) ideas to organize the spectral picture. During training, these layers often develop heavy-tailed power-law (PL) spectra, and the strongest checkpoints often appear near the PL exponent $\alpha\approx 2$. This pattern raises a simple question: why should this value mark a well-trained layer? The answer is both statistical and physical. Our statistical hypothesis is that $\alpha=2$ marks the boundary between stable if not optimal learning and overfitting via non-self-averaging. The physical answer is that the same value makes the spectral energy gain scale evenly across logarithmic spectral scales, which is the proposed Wilsonian marginal condition. We then apply the ERG trace-log condition, the proposed SETOL theory, which we justify by taking volume-preserving, scale-invariant transformations to remove arbitrary layer scale along the proposed RG flow. The resulting spectral RG flow connects the HTSR and SETOL theories to optimizers such as Muon, and motivates a new optimizer extension, WW-PGD. Together, these results point toward a spectral Renormalization-Group view of learning, in which NNs may approach shared spectral structure in the settings studied here.

Authors: Angana Borah, Zoha Hayat Bhatti, Zhijing Jin, Rada Mihalcea

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent settings, exhibiting emergent collective behaviors, yet the role of curiosity as a driver of such dynamics remains understudied. We introduce CuriousTogether, a framework evaluating curiosity-driven multi-agent LLM interactions across three complementary dimensions: novelty-seeking, epistemic and social curiosity. Across eight tasks spanning reasoning, creative generation, and social bias mitigation, curiosity-guided multi-agent settings yield improvements over non-curious ones on seven out of eight tasks, with gains of up to 20.6% in story generation (semantic diversity) and a 14.9 pp reduction in stereotype rates. Finally, we show that inference-time multi-agent curiosity-based approach offers favorable quality-token trade-offs, compared with single-agent baselines. Together, these findings support curiosity-guided interaction as a practical mechanism for improving multi-agent collaboration, with downstream benefits for reasoning, creative, and societal tasks.

replace-cross Iterative Gumbel Planning for Continuous Control

Authors: Shaohuai Liu, Weirui Ye, Yilun Du, Le Xie

Abstract: Search-based planning remains a powerful policy improvement paradigm in reinforcement learning, driving milestone successes in discrete domains. However, extending this paradigm to continuous control exposes a structural mismatch: search evaluates a finite set of action candidates, whereas continuous RL typically relies on Gaussian policies. We show that winner-action distillation into a continuous Gaussian induces a \textit{projection error} and a stepwise variance-expanding gradient tendency when the selected action is sufficiently far from the current mean. Empirically, this tendency is associated with elevated terminal variance and a return gap on the evaluated tasks. To address this, we propose \textbf{Iterative Gumbel Planning (IGP)}. By adopting a per-dimension factorized categorical policy, IGP naturally aligns the policy support with the search candidates, eliminating the projection error and reducing the residual approximation to a bounded discretization error. To bypass the combinatorial explosion of high-dimensional discrete action spaces, IGP pairs Gumbel Top-$K$ candidate sampling with an MPPI-style multi-round iterative search to refine the policy at each state. Empirically, IGP achieves state-of-the-art aggregate performance and sample efficiency on DMControl and HumanoidBench. Furthermore, it produces more stable action sequences with lower temporal variance, demonstrating its practical potential for real-world robotic deployment.

Authors: Thapelo Sindane, Unarine Netshifhefhe, Vukosi Marivate, Abiodun Modupe, Herkulaas Combrink, Ketro Sithole

Abstract: Recent advancements in sign language processing can be attributed to developments in both large-scale dataset collection and algorithmic complexity in areas such as pose estimation techniques, signal processing through Transformer models, and model optimization. Specifically, we automatically collect SASL video content using public resources such as news broadcasts. Additionally, we convert their accompanying audio streams into text using available Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) tools. Furthermore, we crop the sign language area in the videos and use gradient descent to locate valleys of non-motion to determine end-of-sentence points within long videos to create our weakly sentence-aligned parallel corpora -- the Maswao (Signs) dataset. Finally, we benchmark the weakly aligned dataset on the task of Sign-to-text Translation using Recurrent Neural Networks and Transformer models to compare their individual intricacies. Our results show that Transformer models proposed in the literature outperform recurrent baselines, with the best-performing Transformer achieving BLEU-1 to BLEU-4 scores of 13.78, 5.21, 2.52, and 1.54, respectively, as well as ChrF = 18.09 and ROUGE-L = 10.51. replace-cross Beyond Accuracy: What Language-Subspace Interventions Capture, Remove, and Improve

Authors: Shaanil Punglia, Dadhichi Telwadkar, Vaibhav Rathore, Siddharth Sureka, Neha Bhargava

Abstract: Language-subspace interventions aim to improve multilingual reasoning by suppressing language-related components in mid-layer activations. Using MATH and MGSM across four open-weight LLMs, we ask three questions: do estimated language subspaces capture translation-associated variation? Does projecting them out remove language information? And do they improve accuracy without compromising response language? For the first question, we build subspaces using the existing Mean-SVD construction and our DiffPCA. Both capture substantial translation-associated variation and show similar patterns across model depth. For the second, we project out each subspace and train linear probes to classify language from the projected activations. The probes still identify language almost perfectly, showing that neither subspace exhaustively captures language information. For the third, we evaluate several intervention configurations on Qwen2.5-7B, by varying subspace creation methods and intervention operators. We find that the intervention that most improves MGSM accuracy sacrifices response-language fidelity, with only one intervention improving joint accuracy$\unicode{x2014}$answers that are correct and in the target language$\unicode{x2014}$slightly, by 1.60 points. Interestingly, subtle changes in the prompt alone can reproduce the accuracy gains albeit with significant damage to language fidelity. We argue that causal interventions must be judged on more than accuracy, to fully account for language substitution trade-offs.

replace-cross DARE: Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement

Authors: Jikwang Lee, Seungwook Jung, Jaechang Nam

Abstract: LLM-based coding agents are primarily evaluated by their solve rates. However, this metric does not reveal how difficult the solved tasks are or account for differences in task difficulty across benchmarks. Determining the difficulty of individual tasks by repeatedly running multiple agents is costly, while a predicted difficulty score alone provides limited guidance on what support an agent needs. To address these challenges, we propose DARE (Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement), which integrates repair-task difficulty measurement, prediction, feature-group contribution analysis, and task-specific repair support. We define continuous task difficulty as the observed failure rate across multiple coding agents. The DARE difficulty model predicts this difficulty using 151 static features extracted from benchmark artifacts. The framework also analyzes the contributions of seven Repair-Task Feature Groups to identify the characteristics most salient to each task and uses this information to select task-specific agent support. We evaluate DARE on four benchmark partitions comprising 2,594 tasks. The difficulty model's aligned predictions achieve Spearman's $\rho$ = .476-.621 on held-out partitions, and the most salient Feature Groups vary across tasks and benchmarks. Furthermore, coding-agent experiments show that matching support to each task's salient Feature Group raises repair success from 54.7% to 79.6% on nine high-difficulty tasks. These findings demonstrate that difficulty prediction and interpretation can lead to practical improvements in automated program repair (APR), while also providing a basis for difficulty-stratified agent evaluation, cross-benchmark comparisons, and identifying areas of repair capability that warrant further improvement.

replace-cross Today's Work, Tomorrow's Workforce: Incentivizing Learning in AI-Assisted Work

Authors: Moritz Diener, Daniel Hendriks, Philipp Spitzer, Gerhard Satzger, Joshua Holstein, Wei Sun

Abstract: At work, people develop skills through experience: novices acquire skills by completing tasks, while experts retain theirs through continued practice. AI assistance changes this process by offering workers a choice between solving tasks independently and accepting the AI's answers. While relying on AI saves immediate effort, it can also displace the practice needed to acquire and retain skills, including those required to oversee the AI. We extend an existing accept-or-solve model to a sequence of tasks in which workers' skills evolve through independent practice and reliance on AI. We compare myopic workers, who maximize immediate utility, with strategic workers, who also value their future skills. We show that novices can become trapped at low skill levels, while experts may lose skill, even as AI-assisted outcome accuracy remains high. Strategic workers, by contrast, may choose to solve independently even when AI is more accurate, recognizing the future value of acquiring and retaining their skills. We then study incentives that reward correct independent work and characterize the payments needed to encourage skill acquisition and retention. We show that independent work can become self-sustaining once workers acquire sufficient skill, eliminating the need for continued incentives. Counterintuitively, supporting a higher skill target can sometimes reduce long-run incentive costs, either by enabling self-sustaining independent work or by lowering the payments needed to maintain proficiency. Our findings thereby shift the focus from whether workers should rely on AI on individual tasks to how organizations can sustain human expertise over time, and which skills are worth investing in.

replace-cross Route First, See Later: Enabling Vision Agents to Reason Scientifically by Separating Routing Policy from Action Spaces

Authors: Shubhabrata Mukherjee, Jeffrey Donatelli, Daniela Ushizima

Abstract: Scientific image-analysis workflows require agents to select among diverse capabilities like description, detection, counting, and segmentation. Current multi-tool architectures conflate intent interpretation with visual execution, masking whether a failure arises from flawed reasoning (misrouting) or failed perception (invalid tool execution). This lack of auditability renders agents unreliable for high-stakes scientific automation. We introduce ZETA, an extensible architecture that operationalizes the decoupling of routing policy from action execution. ZETA resolves scientific intent before invoking specialized visual tools, treating measurement as a deterministic operation grounded in explicit geometry rather than generative output. This architecture exposes the "black box" of agent decision-making, allowing routing correctness and downstream execution to be evaluated as independent failure modes. To our knowledge, we provide the first controlled study in scientific vision-agent routing that isolates explicit routing-policy specification while holding the legal action interface fixed. Evaluation on a zero-shot benchmark of 51 scientific-intent queries shows that explicit routing semantics can substantially improve routing accuracy, particularly at semantically adjacent action boundaries, although improvements vary across models. For Qwen 3.8, explicit routing-policy specification improves segmentation routing accuracy from 6.2% to 75.0% and detection routing accuracy from 60.0% to 100%. ZETA's execution traces make routing decisions and downstream artifacts separately observable, providing a basis for distinguishing intent-selection errors from visual execution failures. ZETA establishes the necessary infrastructure to isolate execution errors from interpretive ones, a critical requirement for deploying reliable scientific vision agents.

replace-cross Oscillatory Phase Coding Emerges in Adaptive RNNs for Flexible Working Memory

Authors: Kehan Lu, Tianhao Chu, Aiersi Tuerhong, Yiwen Sun, Huan Luo, Fang Fang, Si Wu

Abstract: Oscillatory phase coding (OPC) is widely observed in neural systems and has been implicated in cognitive functions such as memory and sequence processing. However, its computational role and neural substrates remain largely unclear. Here, we studied OPC by training recurrent neural networks (RNNs) with adaptation to perform sequence working memory tasks with variable delays. After training, we found that OPC emerges in the RNN spontaneously: the memorized items recur at distinct phases of a shared rhythm, with oscillation amplitude encoding item identity and phase encoding serial order. Ablation studies identified three key elements required for OPC: adaptation in neural dynamics, variable delay in memory retention, and flexibility in reading out information. Neuron-type analysis revealed the emergence of content and time cells in the trained RNN, and they process item identity and sequence order separately. Recurrent connectivity analysis revealed phase-organized connections among time cells and structured connections within digit-selective content-cell groups. Inspired by these findings, we proposed a neural circuit model which reproduces OPC through interactions between content units and an adaptive time ring. Together, this study reveals how OPC arises from network structures and task demands, offers insights into the implementation and computational mechanisms of OPC in biological systems, and inspires us to develop computational models for human-like cognitive functions.

replace-cross Archetypal contexts in the neurons of language models: an architecture for intelligence?

Authors: David Nordfors

Abstract: We validate our previous hypothesis that a language model holds relational patterns apart from the subject domains that instantiate them (Nordfors, 2026). These archetypal contexts are patterns of relations that people recognize across many fields, such as the tipping point or winner takes all, held once and applied across domains. We show this inside the model, Qwen3.8-27B. One set of neurons carries the archetype, say the tipping point; another carries the topic, say politics, ecology or cell biology; a third carries the language, such as English, Spanish or Chinese. Silence the tipping point's neurons, and the model no longer identifies the pattern but still knows what the text is about. Silence the topic's, and it loses the topic but still names the pattern. In the neuron maps the layers organize themselves into a sandwich: the outer layers carry languages, the layers inside them topics, the layers at the heart archetypes. The sandwich emerges spontaneously during pretraining and appears in every model we mapped. Archetypes are the architecture for analogy, and we strongly suggest that this structure of archetypes and topics is the mechanism of a language model's intelligence: we see it self-organize as the model is trained. The neurons, the texts and the code are released with this paper as its reproduction package, so that anyone can repeat the test inside the model.
